    (function () {
        if ( typeof window.CustomEvent === "function" ) return false;
        function CustomEvent ( event, params ) {
            params = params || { bubbles: false, cancelable: false, detail: null };
            var evt = document.createEvent( 'CustomEvent' );
            evt.initCustomEvent( event, params.bubbles, params.cancelable, params.detail );
            return evt;
        }

        CustomEvent.prototype = window.Event.prototype;

        window.CustomEvent = CustomEvent;
    })();
</script>

                ].filter(function (s) {
                    if (s.src === null) return false;
                    if (moduleSupport && s.nomodule) return false;
                    return !(!moduleSupport && s.module);
                }),

                register: function (value) {
                    this.registered.push(value);
                },

                ready: function () {
                    if (this.registered.length === this.scripts.length) {
                        this.registered.forEach(function (fn) {
                            if (typeof fn === 'function') {
                                setTimeout(fn, 0); 
                            }
                        });
                        this.ready = function () {};
                    }
                },

                insert: function (s) {
                    var t = d.getElementById('js-position' + this.index);
                    if (t && t.insertAdjacentElement) {
                        t.insertAdjacentElement('afterend', s);
                    } else {
                        d.head.appendChild(s);
                    }
                    ++this.index;
                },

                createScript: function (script, beforeLoad) {
                    var s = d.createElement('script');
                    s.id = 'js-position' + (this.index + 1);
                    s.setAttribute('data-test', script.test);
                    if (beforeLoad) {
                        s.defer = 'defer';
                        s.onload = function () {
                            if (script.noinit) {
                                loader.register(true);
                            }
                            if (d.readyState === 'interactive' || d.readyState === 'complete') {
                                loader.ready();
                            }
                        };
                    } else {
                        s.async = 'async';
                    }
                    s.src = script.src;
                    return s;
                },

                init: function () {
                    this.scripts.forEach(function (s) {
                        loader.insert(loader.createScript(s, true));
                    });

    <script type="application/ld+json">{"mainEntity":{"headline":"Multimodal oral cancer detection with embedding-level oversampling of fused image and clinical data","description":"Oral cancer is a highly aggressive disease that is often detected too late, resulting in poor survival outcomes. Screening methods are limited by subjective assessment and by the scarcity of confirmed cancer cases in real-world datasets. We developed a multimodal approach that combines photographic features with basic clinical information to improve early identification of malignant lesions while addressing the strong imbalance typically found in medical datasets. Multiple machine learning models were trained on a dataset of annotated oral images paired with patient metadata, using synthetic sample generation techniques to improve representation of cancer cases. Our best configuration achieved good macro F1 and Area Under Receiver Operating Characteristic, outperforming image-only baselines. The improvements indicate that integrating contextual patient information was associated with improved class-balanced performance under the evaluated protocol. These findings reflect performance differences under the evaluated multimodal protocol and imbalance learning for assisting clinicians in earlier recognition of oral cancer. Our key contribution lies in embedding-level oversampling within a fused image–metadata space, which preserves latent semantics while improving minority-class sensitivity. These results arise from a tightly controlled, single-center experimental setup and are best understood as exploratory insights into the methodology, rather than as evidence that can be readily extended to broader clinical contexts. These findings reflect performance differences within the evaluated experimental setting. Metadata integration is treated as a structural component of multimodal representation within a constrained experimental     <meta name="dc.description" content="Oral cancer is a highly aggressive disease that is often detected too late, resulting in poor survival outcomes. Screening methods are limited by subjective assessment and by the scarcity of confirmed cancer cases in real-world datasets. We developed a multimodal approach that combines photographic features with basic clinical information to improve early identification of malignant lesions while addressing the strong imbalance typically found in medical datasets. Multiple machine learning models were trained on a dataset of annotated oral images paired with patient metadata, using synthetic sample generation techniques to improve representation of cancer cases. Our best configuration achieved good macro F1 and Area Under Receiver Operating Characteristic, outperforming image-only baselines. The improvements indicate that integrating contextual patient information was associated with improved class-balanced performance under the evaluated protocol. These findings reflect performance differences under the evaluated multimodal protocol and imbalance learning for assisting clinicians in earlier recognition of oral cancer. Our key contribution lies in embedding-level oversampling within a fused image&#8211;metadata space, which preserves latent semantics while improving minority-class sensitivity. These results arise from a tightly controlled, single-center experimental setup and are best understood as exploratory insights into the methodology, rather than as evidence that can be readily extended to broader clinical contexts. These findings reflect performance differences within the evaluated experimental setting. Metadata integration is treated as a structural component of multimodal representation within a constrained experimental framework."/>

    <meta name="description" content="Oral cancer is a highly aggressive disease that is often detected too late, resulting in poor survival outcomes. Screening methods are limited by subjective assessment and by the scarcity of confirmed cancer cases in real-world datasets. We developed a multimodal approach that combines photographic features with basic clinical information to improve early identification of malignant lesions while addressing the strong imbalance typically found in medical datasets. Multiple machine learning models were trained on a dataset of annotated oral images paired with patient metadata, using synthetic sample generation techniques to improve representation of cancer cases. Our best configuration achieved good macro F1 and Area Under Receiver Operating Characteristic, outperforming image-only baselines. The improvements indicate that integrating contextual patient information was associated with improved class-balanced performance under the evaluated protocol. These findings reflect performance differences under the evaluated multimodal protocol and imbalance learning for assisting clinicians in earlier recognition of oral cancer. Our key contribution lies in embedding-level oversampling within a fused image&#8211;metadata space, which preserves latent semantics while improving minority-class sensitivity. These results arise from a tightly controlled, single-center experimental setup and are best understood as exploratory insights into the methodology, rather than as evidence that can be readily extended to broader clinical contexts. These findings reflect performance differences within the evaluated experimental setting. Metadata integration is treated as a structural component of multimodal representation within a constrained experimental framework."/>

                <div class="c-article-body">
                    <section aria-labelledby="Abs1" data-title="Abstract" lang="en"><div class="c-article-section" id="Abs1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Abs1">Abstract</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Abs1-content"><p>Oral cancer is a highly aggressive disease that is often detected too late, resulting in poor survival outcomes. Screening methods are limited by subjective assessment and by the scarcity of confirmed cancer cases in real-world datasets. We developed a multimodal approach that combines photographic features with basic clinical information to improve early identification of malignant lesions while addressing the strong imbalance typically found in medical datasets. Multiple machine learning models were trained on a dataset of annotated oral images paired with patient metadata, using synthetic sample generation techniques to improve representation of cancer cases. Our best configuration achieved good macro F1 and Area Under Receiver Operating Characteristic, outperforming image-only baselines. The improvements indicate that integrating contextual patient information was associated with improved class-balanced performance under the evaluated protocol. These findings reflect performance differences under the evaluated multimodal protocol and imbalance learning for assisting clinicians in earlier recognition of oral cancer. Our key contribution lies in embedding-level oversampling within a fused image–metadata space, which preserves latent semantics while improving minority-class sensitivity. These results arise from a tightly controlled, single-center experimental setup and are best understood as exploratory insights into the methodology, rather than as evidence that can be readily extended to broader clinical contexts. These findings reflect performance differences within the evaluated experimental setting. Metadata integration is treated as a structural component of multimodal representation within a constrained experimental framework.</p></div></div></section>

                        <div class="main-content">
                            
                                <section data-title="Introduction"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec1">Introduction</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec1-content"><p>Oral Cancer (OCA) represents a significant global health challenge, characterised by high incidence and mortality rates<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 1" title="Rivera, C. Essentials of oral cancer. Int. J. Clin. Exp. Pathol. 8, 11884 (2015)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR1" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e531">1</a></sup>. The World Health Organization reports that this disease causes approximately 200,000 deaths annually<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2" title="World Health Organization. Oral cancer statistics. WHO (2023). Accessed: 2025–10–29." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR2" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e535">2</a></sup>. It is among the most prevalent head and neck malignancies worldwide, with late-stage detection strongly affecting prognosis and survival. Traditional diagnosis relies on visual inspection and biopsy confirmation, which are resource-intensive and prone to inter- observer variability<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 3" title="McGurk, M., Chan, C., Jones, J., O’regan, E. &amp; Sherriff, M. Delay in diagnosis and its effect on outcome in head and neck cancer. Br. J. Oral Maxillofac. Surg. 43, 281–284 (2005)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e539">3</a></sup>. This disease represents a significant global health burden, ranking among the most common cancers in low and middle income countries<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 4" title="Ramachandran, S. Oral cancer: Recent breakthroughs in pathology and therapeutic approaches. Oral Oncol. Rep. &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.oor.2024.100678&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e543">4</a></sup>. Early detection is critical because the 5-year survival rate drops drastically, from over 80% in early stages to less than 30% in advanced stages<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 5" title="Balapure, A., Dubey, S. K., Javed, A., Chattopadhyay, S. &amp; Goel, S. A review: Early detection of oral cancer biomarkers using microfluidic colorimetric point-of-care devices. Anal. Methods 16, 6098–6118 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e547">5</a></sup>. Despite being accessible to visual and tactile examination, oral cancers are frequently diagnosed late due to subtle early-stage symptoms and limited screening programs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 6" title="Tsai, E., Walker, B. &amp; Wu, S.-C. Can oral cancer screening reduce late-stage diagnosis, treatment delay and mortality? A population-based study in Taiwan. BMJ Open 14, e086588 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e552">6</a></sup>. Early identification not only enhances survival but also reduces treatment-related morbidity and healthcare costs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 7" title="Sahoo, R. K. et al. Diagnostic performance of artificial intelligence in detecting oral potentially malignant disorders and oral cancer using medical diagnostic imaging: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Front. Oral Heal. 5, 1494867 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e556">7</a></sup>. Integrating Artificial Intelligence (AI) driven diagnostic tools into clinical workflows has the potential to assist clinicians in identifying malignant lesions earlier and 1 more accurately, thereby enhancing prognostic outcomes and guiding timely interventions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="González-Ruiz, I., Ramos-Garcia, P., Ruiz-Avila, I. &amp; Gonzalez-Moles, M. A. Early diagnosis of oral cancer: A complex polyhedral problem with a difficult solution. Cancers (Basel) 15, 3270 (2023)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e560">8</a></sup>. Although oral cancers are accessible to visual inspection, imaging alone often fails to capture the full complexity of patient risk. Subtle clinical manifestations can be indistinguishable without contextual knowledge such as demographics, lifestyle factors, and anatomical site, motivating the integration of multimodal information. These challenges have prompted the exploration of artificial-intelligence-assisted diagnostics that can detect malignant patterns earlier and more consistently.</p><p>Multimodal data integration in medical diagnosis leverages the complementary strengths of diverse information sources to enhance discriminative diagnostic accuracy and robustness<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 9" title="Boehm, K. M. et al. Multimodal data integration using machine learning improves risk stratification of high-grade serous ovarian cancer. Nat. cancer 3, 723–733 (2022)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR9" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e567">9</a></sup>. In the context of OCA detection, imaging data, such as intraoral photographs, cytology slides, or histopathology images, provide rich visual features that capture morphological changes associated with malignancy<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10" title="Kumar, S., Rani, S., Sharma, S. &amp; Min, H. Multimodality fusion aspects of medical diagnosis: A comprehensive review. Bioengineering (Basel) 11, 1233 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e571">10</a></sup>. However, visual data alone may be insufficient to fully characterize patient-specific risk. Integrating patient metadata, such as age, sex, and clinical risk factors, provides additional contextual information that may support clinically informed decision-making; however, this integration does not in itself constitute model interpretability as defined in the explainable AI literature<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Simon, B. D., Ozyoruk, K. B., Gelikman, D. G., Harmon, S. A. &amp; Türkbey, B. The future of multimodal artificial intelligence models for integrating imaging and clinical metadata: A narrative review. Diagn. Interv. Radiol. 31, 303 (2025)." href="#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e575">11</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Cimino, M. G. et al. Explainable screening of oral cancer via deep learning and case-based reasoning. Smart Health 35, 100538 (2025)." href="#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e575_1">12</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Guidotti, R. et al. A survey of methods for explaining black box models. ACM computing surveys (CSUR) 51, 1–42 (2018)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e578">13</a></sup> . By combining these modalities, diagnostic models can learn richer feature representations that account for both visual and non-visual cues, leading to more nuanced and individualized predictions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14" title="Duan, J., Xiong, J., Li, Y. &amp; Ding, W. Deep learning based multimodal biomedical data fusion: An overview and comparative review. Inf. Fusion 112, 102536 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e582">14</a></sup>. Recent advances in Deep Learning (DL) have enabled effective fusion strategies for multimodal biomedical data, paving the way for precision diagnostics in oral oncology<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 15" title="Chen, J. et al. A deep learning-based multimodal medical imaging model for breast cancer screening. Sci. Reports 15, 14696 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e586">15</a></sup>. However, multimodal datasets often heighten class imbalance. While abundant healthy or benign samples exist across modalities, malignant and premalignant cases remain limited, producing highly skewed distributions that compromise model sensitivity in exactly the cases of greatest clinical importance<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Tasci, E., Zhuge, Y., Camphausen, K. &amp; Krauze, A. V. Bias and class imbalance in oncologic data—Towards inclusive and transferrable AI in large scale oncology data sets. Cancers 14, 2897 (2022)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e591">16</a></sup>. Recent multimodal cancer studies have achieved Area Under Curve (AUROC) improvements of 10–20 % and accuracies above 90 %, underscoring the superior diagnostic performance of integrated data approaches compared with unimodal models. Recent transformer-based multimodal systems have demonstrated strong performance in medical imaging tasks; however, such architectures typically require large-scale datasets and extensive computational resources. The present study intentionally restricts architectural complexity in order to isolate imbalance mitigation behaviour within fused embeddings under constrained data conditions <sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Huang, J., Zhou, J., Tang, Z., Lin, J. &amp; Chen, C.Y.-C. Tmbl: Transformer-based multimodal binding learning model for multimodal sentiment analysis. Knowl. Based Syst. 285, 111346 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e595">17</a></sup>. Nevertheless, no study has systematically examined imbalance-aware learning within fused image and metadata embeddings for oral cancer detection. We address this gap by evaluating embedding-level imbalance-handling approaches within a multimodal fusion framework and analyzing their effect on class-balanced performance measures for oral cancer classification.</p><p>Machine Learning (ML) and DL have emerged as promising tools to assist in early detection through pattern recognition of oral lesions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Ormeño-Arriagada, P. et al. Early oral cancer detection with ai: Design and implementation of a deep learning image-based chatbot. Appl. Sci. 15, 10792 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e602">18</a></sup>. Their performance often suffers from the substantial class imbalance typical of medical datasets, where early-stage or malignant lesions represent only a small subset of cases. This imbalance is particularly problematic in diagnostic tasks, as positive disease instances are considerably rarer than healthy or benign samples, leading to reduced model sensitivity for clinically critical cases<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19" title="Altalhan, M., Algarni, A. &amp; Alouane, M.T.-H. Imbalanced data problem in machine learning: A review. IEEE Access &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/access.2025.3531662&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e606">19</a></sup>. It also can bias model learning toward majority classes, reducing sensitivity for clinically critical cancer cases. In oral cancer datasets, for example, healthy and benign samples often outnumber malignant or pre-malignant cases by large margins<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Song, B. et al. Classification of imbalanced oral cancer image data from high-risk population. J. biomedical optics 26, 105001–105001 (2021)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e610">20</a></sup>. Class imbalance in cancer datasets biases models toward majority classes, resulting in high accuracy but reduced sensitivity for detecting malignant and pre-malignant cases.<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Salmi, M., Atif, D., Oliva, D., Abraham, A. &amp; Ventura, S. Handling imbalanced medical datasets: Review of a decade of research. Artif. Intell. Rev. 57, 273 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e614">21</a></sup>. Such misclassifications are unacceptable in clinical settings, where false negatives can delay diagnosis and worsen outcomes<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22" title="Fan, M.-s et al. Risk factors associated with false negative rate of sentinel lymph node biopsy in endometrial cancer: A systematic review and meta-analysis. Front. Oncol. 14, 1391267 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR22" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e618">22</a></sup>. Moreover, imbalanced data can distort decision boundaries, hinder generalization, and reduce the reliability of performance metrics like accuracy. Addressing this issue requires specialized techniques such as data resampling, cost-sensitive learning, or hybrid augmentation strategies that preserve clinical validity while improving model fairness and sensitivity<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Ramyachitra, D. &amp; Manikandan, P. Imbalanced dataset classification and solutions: A review. Int. J. Comput. Bus. Res. (IJCBR) 5, 1–29 (2014)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e623">23</a></sup>. Previous research in oral cancer diagnosis has either analysed imaging and patient metadata separately or relied heavily on raw image augmentation to balance datasets<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Piyarathne, N. et al. A comprehensive dataset of annotated oral cavity images for diagnosis of oral cancer and oral potentially malignant disorders. Oral Oncol. 156, 106946 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e627">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25" title="Araújo, A. L. D., Pedroso, C. M., Vargas, P. A., Lopes, M. A. &amp; Santos-Silva, A. R. Advancing oral cancer diagnosis and risk assessment with artificial intelligence: A review. Explor. Digit. Heal. Technol. 3, 101147 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR25" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e630">25</a></sup>. Systematic evaluation of oversampling in fused multimodal embeddings remains limited, hindering balanced integration of visual and contextual data<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26" title="Yoshitsugu, K., Kishimoto, K. &amp; Takemura, T. Improving the sensitivity of unbalanced medical image classification by random up-sampling numerical features. In Proceedings of the 2024 6th International Conference on Intelligent Medicine and Image Processing, 15–21 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e634">26</a></sup>. Consequently, imbalance-aware multimodal learning could generate representative minority samples and enhance sensitivity in early oral cancer detection<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="Warin, K. &amp; Suebnukarn, S. Deep learning in oral cancer-A systematic review. BMC Oral Health 24, 212 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e638">27</a></sup>. Within this context, recent studies have explored multimodal learning frameworks for oral cancer detection by integrating imaging data with clinical or contextual information<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Devindi, G. A. I. et al. Multimodal deep convolutional neural network pipeline for AI-assisted early detection of oral cancer. IEEE Access 12, 124375–124390. &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/ACCESS.2024.3454338&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2024)." href="#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e642">28</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Mitbander, R. et al. Development and evaluation of an automated multimodal mobile detection of oral cancer imaging system to aid in risk-based management of oral mucosal lesions. Cancer Prev. Res. 18, 197–207 (2025)." href="#ref-CR29" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e642_1">29</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Zhang, J., Tian, Y., Song, B. &amp; Lee, E. J. Multimodal learning for enhanced detection in oral cancer screening. In 2025 IEEE 22nd International Symposium on Biomedical Imaging (ISBI), 1–5 (IEEE, 2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e645">30</a></sup>. These works primarily focus on end-to-end system design and performance optimization across heterogeneous acquisition settings. In contrast, the present study investigates how embedding-level imbalance mitigation interacts with multimodal fusion under limited and imbalanced data conditions, providing complementary insight into model stability and minority-class sensitivity rather than proposing a new screening system.</p><p>To mitigate class imbalance in training datasets, several oversampling techniques have been developed to synthetically generate new samples for underrepresented classes<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 31" title="Kim, M. &amp; Hwang, K.-B. An empirical evaluation of sampling methods for the classification of imbalanced data. PLoS ONE 17, e0271260 (2022)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR31" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e652">31</a></sup>. Among the most widely adopted are Class Weighting (CW), Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique (SMOTE) and Adaptive Synthetic Sampling (ADASYN). SMOTE generates synthetic data by interpolating between existing minority class samples, effectively introducing plausible intermediate representations in the feature space without simply duplicating original instances<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 32" title="Tajali, A. et al. The impactness of smote as imbalance class handling for myocardial infarction complication classification using machine learning approach with data imputation and hyperparameter. Indonesian J. Electron. Electromed. Eng. Med. Informatics 6, 227–239 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR32" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e656">32</a></sup>. This helps the model learn smoother class boundaries and reduces overfitting. ADASYN extends this idea by focusing the oversampling process on harder-to-learn minority samples—those near class boundaries, thereby adaptively shifting the decision boundary to improve minority class detection<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 33" title="Hairani, H., Widiyaningtyas, T. &amp; Prasetya, D. D. Addressing class imbalance of health data: A systematic literature review on modified synthetic minority oversampling technique (SMOTE) strategies. JOIV Int. J. Informatics Vis. 8, 1310–1318 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR33" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e660">33</a></sup>. Both techniques are particularly well-suited for tabular data or embedding spaces, such as those formed by deep learning models, where they help balance datasets and enhance model sensitivity to rare but clinically important cases. Additionally, embedding-space.</p><p>oversampling preserves the latent semantic structure of multimodal representations, ensuring consistency between visual and contextual features. It also offers a computationally efficient alternative to pixel-level augmentation, reducing processing overhead while maintaining meaningful data variability.</p><p>This investigation aims to evaluate the directional improvement of multimodal fusion in addressing class imbalance in oral cancer classification<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 34" title="Gholampour, S. Impact of nature of medical data on machine and deep learning for imbalanced datasets: Clinical validity of SMOTE is questionable. Mach. Learn. Knowl. Extr. 6, 827–841 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR34" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e671">34</a></sup>. Our approach proposes a multimodal learning approach that integrates image embeddings with patient metadata to improve early oral cancer detection. Combining complementary information sources and handling data imbalance will enhance model reliability and clinical applicability. We hypothesise that applying oversampling within a fused image–metadata embedding space improves minority-class classification compared with unimodal or unbalanced baselines<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 35" title="Mujahid, M. et al. Data oversampling and imbalanced datasets: an investigation of performance for machine learning and feature engineering. J. Big Data 11, 87 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e675">35</a></sup>. Specifically, we propose a fusion strategy that combines image embeddings, extracted from a pretrained Convolutional Neural Network (CNN), with structured patient metadata to form unified feature vectors. These fused representations are then used as input for oversampling techniques such as SMOTE and ADASYN, applied directly in the embedding space. The objective is twofold: first, to generate synthetic representations of minority classes that preserve both visual characteristics and associated clinical context; and second, to improve classifier performance, with particular emphasis on sensitivity to underrepresented conditions such as oral cancer. However, most existing studies focus solely on image data and overlook complementary clinical metadata such as age, sex, and risk factors. Persistent class imbalance in available datasets further limits model generalisation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 36" title="Gurcan, F. &amp; Soylu, A. Learning from imbalanced data: Integration of advanced resampling techniques and machine learning models for enhanced cancer diagnosis and prognosis. Cancers 16, 3417 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR36" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e679">36</a></sup>. In summary, the study evaluates the combined effect of multimodal fusion and embedding-level imbalance mitigation on oral cancer classification under limited and imbalanced data conditions. By comparing image-only and multimodal configurations with and without oversampling, it provides evidence that balancing fused representations improves sensitivity to minority cancer cases. These findings support the use of embedding-level augmentation as a practical and reproducible strategy in multimodal oral oncology research. This study provides an empirical evaluation of embedding-level imbalance mitigation for oversampling within multimodal feature spaces, reducing reliance on direct image-level augmentation, together with empirical evidence that balancing fused embeddings enhances recall and macro F1 for minority oral cancer cases. In addition, a reproducible evaluation on real-world clinical data demonstrates the diagnostic relevance of embedding-level augmentation in AI-based oral oncology. By addressing data imbalance and the limited integration of patient metadata, the proposed approach improves sensitivity to rare malignant lesions and establishes a methodological foundation for the development of more equitable and generalisable multimodal models in oral cancer research. Accordingly, this work contributes a structured configuration comparison under constrained experimental conditions, examining how fusion and imbalance handling strategies influence class-balanced performance within a fixed experimental setup. Moreover, we examine the behaviour of embedding-level imbalance mitigation within a multimodal image–metadata classification framework under constrained data conditions, with the objective of evaluating whether latent-space resampling after fusion influences class-balanced performance within a fixed architectural configuration. In this context, the present study does not aim to establish state-of-the-art performance relative to contemporary transformer-based multimodal architectures, but instead isolates the behaviour of embedding-level resampling within a fixed and tractable architectural configuration. Indeed, direct benchmarking against large-scale transformer systems would require substantially larger datasets and fundamentally different experimental objectives, and therefore falls outside the scope of the current work.</p><p>The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="section anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Sec2">Related Work</a> situates this study within the existing literature on oral cancer detection, multimodal learning, and imbalance mitigation strategies, highlighting current approaches and identifying key methodological gaps that motivate our work. In Section <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="section anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Sec3">Methods</a>, we present the dataset characteristics, preprocessing pipeline, and the multimodal fusion framework that integrates image embeddings with clinical metadata. Section <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="section anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Sec12">Results</a> reports the comparative performance of four classifiers under different imbalance-handling strategies, including CW, SMOTE, and ADASYN. Finally, Section <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="section anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Sec18">Discussion</a> interprets the findings in light of existing literature, examining the clinical and methodological implications of embedding-level oversampling for oral cancer detection.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Related work"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec2-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec2">Related work</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec2-content"><p>Recent advances in medical image analysis have been largely driven by DL architectures, particularly CNN and hybrid attention-based models. Numerous studies have demonstrated high performance in cancer detection and segmentation tasks through increasingly sophisticated architectures and optimization strategies. For example, segmentation-focused approaches such as CICADA (UCX) achieve high Dice and AUC scores by integrating feature selection and optimization techniques into U-Net-based pipelines<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 37" title="Singh, D. P., Banerjee, T., Kour, P., Swain, D. &amp; Narayan, Y. Cicada (ucx): A novel approach for automated breast cancer classification through aggressiveness delineation. Comput. Biol. Chem. 115, 108368 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR37" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e706">37</a></sup>. Similarly, hybrid DL models incorporating attention mechanisms and multi-scale feature extraction have been successfully applied to thyroid ultrasound, liver tumor, and prostate cancer segmentation, improving robustness against noise and variability in imaging conditions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Banerjee, T. et al. A novel hybrid deep learning approach combining deep feature attention and statistical validation for enhanced thyroid ultrasound segmentation. Sci. Rep. 15, 27207 (2025)." href="#ref-CR38" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e710">38</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Banerjee, T. et al. A novel unified inception-u-net hybrid gravitational optimization model (uigo) incorporating automated medical image segmentation and feature selection for liver tumor detection. Sci. Reports 15, 29908 (2025)." href="#ref-CR39" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e710_1">39</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 40" title="Narayan, Y. et al. A comparative evaluation of deep learning architectures for prostate cancer segmentation: Introducing trionixnet with n-core multi-attention mechanism. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 1–40 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR40" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e713">40</a></sup>.</p><p>Beyond segmentation, classification-oriented frameworks have explored combinations of DL and optimization algorithms to enhance predictive performance. Hybrid architectures integrating feature selection and attention mechanisms, such as AKAttNet combined with electromagnetic interaction-based optimization, have demonstrated strong results in neurodevelopmental disorder classification tasks<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 41" title="Banerjee, T. Electromagnetic interaction algorithm (eia)-based feature selection with adaptive kernel attention network (akattnet) for autism spectrum disorder classification. Int. J. Dev. Neurosci. 85, e70034 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR41" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e720">41</a></sup>. In oncology contexts, multimodal and hybrid learning strategies have also been explored for tasks such as.</p><p>drug response prediction, where heterogeneous data sources are integrated to improve predictive performance<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 42" title="Singh, D. P. et al. A comprehensive study of enhanced computational approaches for breast cancer classification: comparative analysis with existing state of the art methods. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 1–29 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR42" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e727">42</a></sup>. Additionally, attention-based and pyramidal architectures have been proposed to improve feature representation and classification accuracy across heterogeneous datasets, often achieving high performance through complex architectural designs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 43" title="Banerjee, T. et al. Pyramidal attention-based t network for brain tumor classification: a comprehensive analysis of transfer learning approaches for clinically reliable and reliable ai hybrid approaches. Sci. Reports 15, 28669 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR43" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e731">43</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 44" title="Pacal, I. &amp; Banerjee, T. Towards accurate and interpretable brain tumor diagnosis: T-fspannet with tri-attribute and pyramidal attention-based feature fusion. Biomed. Signal Process. Control 113, 108852 (2026)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR44" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e734">44</a></sup>.</p><p>In parallel, several works have explored the role of preprocessing, feature engineering, and model selection in improving classification outcomes in medical imaging. Comparative studies across architectures such as ResNet, DenseNet, and MobileNet highlight the importance of representation learning and optimization strategies in achieving high accuracy in tasks such as diabetic retinopathy detection<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 45" title="Singh, D. P. et al. A comprehensive study on deep learning models for the detection of diabetic retinopathy using pathological images. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 33, 503–532 (2026)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR45" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e741">45</a></sup>. Similarly, optimized hybrid frameworks for breast cancer classification demonstrate how feature selection and attention-based mechanisms can enhance predictive performance under varying imaging conditions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 42" title="Singh, D. P. et al. A comprehensive study of enhanced computational approaches for breast cancer classification: comparative analysis with existing state of the art methods. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 1–29 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR42" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e745">42</a></sup>. Reviews of deep learning and transformer-based methods further emphasize the growing dominance of large-scale architectures, particularly Vision Transformers and hybrid models, which leverage extensive datasets and computational resources to achieve superior performance<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 46" title="Banerjee, T., Singh, D. P. &amp; Kour, P. Advances in deep neural, transformer learning, and kernel-based methods for diabetic retinopathy detection: a comprehensive review. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 1–49 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR46" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e749">46</a></sup>.</p><p>Explainability has also emerged as a relevant direction in medical AI, with approaches such as attention visualization and Grad-CAM being applied to improve interpretability in skin cancer detection models<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 47" title="Banerjee, T. Comparing bipartite convoluted and attention-driven methods for skin cancer detection: A review of explainable ai and transfer learning strategies. Arch. Comput. Methods Eng. 1–25 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR47" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e757">47</a></sup>. However, systematic reviews indicate that despite strong reported performance, a persistent gap remains between experimental results and real-world clinical applicability, often due to limited external validation, lack of interpretability, and insufficient evaluation under heterogeneous conditions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 48" title="Banerjee, T. &amp; Paçal, ˙I. A systematic review of machine learning in heart disease prediction. Turk. J. Biol. 49, 600–634 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR48" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e761">48</a></sup>.</p><p>With respect to class imbalance, most existing approaches rely on data-level or algorithm-level strategies, including oversampling methods such as SMOTE and ADASYN, cost-sensitive learning, and data augmentation techniques<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 49" title="Sun, Z. et al. K-means-smote-ala-xgboost: Diagnosis of faults in hot-rolled strip steel plates. Alex. Eng. J. 142, 165–184 (2026)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR49" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e768">49</a></sup>. These methods are well established in structured and tabular domains and are typically applied prior to training or within the learning objective. However, their application in multimodal medical imaging—particularly in feature fusion settings—remains comparatively underexplored<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 50" title="Ahmed, A., Zeng, X., Xi, R., Hou, M. &amp; Shah, S. A. Enhancing multimodal medical image analysis with slice-fusion: A novel fusion approach to address modality imbalance. Comput. Methods Programs Biomed. 261, 108615 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR50" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e772">50</a></sup>. Existing studies generally address imbalance at the input or model level without explicitly analysing its interaction with fused latent representations.</p><p>Recent advances in biomedical AI increasingly emphasize explainability, collaborative validation, and clinical translation. Explainability frameworks, including SHAP and Grad-CAM, have been applied to improve transparency in diagnostic models, particularly in oncology imaging tasks<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 51" title="Lucas, M., Lerma, M., Furst, J. &amp; Raicu, D. Rsi-grad-cam: Visual explanations from deep networks via riemann-stieltjes integrated gradient-based localization. In International Symposium on Visual Computing, 262–274 (Springer, 2022)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR51" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e779">51</a></sup>. In parallel, federated learning approaches enable collaborative model training across institutions without direct data sharing, addressing privacy and data governance constraints in clinical environments<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 52" title="Prendin, F. et al. The importance of interpreting machine learning models for blood glucose prediction in diabetes: An analysis using shap. Sci. Rep. 13, 16865 (2023)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR52" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e783">52</a></sup>. Clinical translation studies further highlight the importance of domain-shift robustness, regulatory compliance, and prospective validation when deploying AI systems in real-world healthcare settings<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 53" title="Olawade, D. B. et al. Artificial intelligence in healthcare delivery: Prospects and pitfalls. J. Medicine, Surgery, Public Heal. 3, 100108 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR53" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e787">53</a></sup>.</p><p>While these developments are critical for translational deployment, the present study isolates imbalance mitigation behaviour within a constrained multimodal configuration. Additionally, the present work adopts a constrained experimental design to isolate the behaviour of embedding-level resampling within a multimodal image–metadata framework. Rather than proposing a novel algorithm or competing with large-scale architectures, this study provides a structured empirical examination of imbalance mitigation after feature fusion under limited data conditions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 54" title="Singh, V. K., Agarwal, D., Gediya, V. K., Rathore, R. S. &amp; Jiang, W. Mitigating class imbalance in forest fire prediction with gan-augmented data fusion. Inf. Fusion 104005 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR54" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e794">54</a></sup>. This positioning allows for controlled analysis of imbalance mitigation effects without introducing confounding factors associated with large-scale architectural variation.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Methods"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec3-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec3">Methods</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec3-content"><p>This section describes the methodological framework developed to improve oral cancer classification through Multimodal Data Fusion and advanced imbalance mitigation. We outline the dataset composition, preprocessing procedures, and feature extraction using pretrained CNN. Subsequently, we detail the integration of image embeddings with structured clinical metadata, the implementation of embedding-level oversampling techniques, and the evaluation protocols used to assess model performance. This framework operationalizes our hypothesis that embedding-level oversampling within a fused multimodal representation is intended to improve minority-class classification under the evaluated conditions. Three configurations were evaluated: (i) image-only classification models, serving as a unimodal baseline; (ii) multimodal fusion models combining image-derived embeddings with clinical metadata, without oversampling; and (iii) multimodal fusion models augmented with embedding-level oversampling to address class imbalance. This staged evaluation design enables a partial component impact analysis by isolating the effects of multimodal integration and oversampling strategies on classification performance. Quantitative differences across these configurations are reported using clinically relevant metrics, providing insight into the relative contribution of each component.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec4">Proposed multimodal classification Pipeline</h3><p>Figure <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Fig1">1</a> illustrates the overall workflow of the proposed multimodal oral cancer classification system. The pipeline begins with a preprocessing stage, where all images are standardised by resizing them to 224 × 224 pixels, adding a uniform border, applying a mild blur to reduce noise, and removing duplicates to ensure data quality.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-1" data-title="Fig. 1"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig1" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 1</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41598-026-52410-0/MediaObjects/41598_2026_52410_Fig1_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-1-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41598-026-52410-0/MediaObjects/41598_2026_52410_Fig1_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 1" loading="lazy" width="685" height="261"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/figures/1" data-track-dest="link:Figure1 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 1" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-1-desc"><p>Overview of the multimodal oral cancer classification pipeline. Images are preprocessed, embedded into 512-dimensional feature vectors, and fused with patient metadata (age, sex, lifestyle risk factors). The dataset is split into 70% training and 30% testing, with imbalance handled using SMOTE, ADASYN, or CW loss. Models are trained and evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, macro F1, AUROC, and Cohen’s Kappa. Preprocessing corresponds to Methods Proposed Multimodal Classification Pipeline, fusion to Methods Vector Fusion, and classification/evaluation to Methods Classifier Models; Evaluation.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>In the feature extraction stage, high-dimensional embeddings (512 features) are generated from the preprocessed images using a CNN. These embeddings are then concatenated with structured patient metadata, including demographic attributes (age and sex) and behavioural risk factors (alcohol consumption, smoking, and betel-quid chewing), resulting in a multimodal feature representation.</p><p>The fused dataset is partitioned into a training set (70%) and a testing set (30%). The reuse of identical held-out test partitions across imbalance strategies was intentionally maintained to enable paired within-split comparison, while recognising that this design does not substitute for broader cross-validation. To address the inherent class imbalance, oversampling methods such as SMOTE and ADASYN, together with CW loss, are applied exclusively to the training data.</p><p>Multiple classifiers are trained on the rebalanced dataset, and model performance is evaluated on the unseen test set. Evaluation metrics include accuracy, precision, recall, macro F1, the area under the receiver operating characteristic (AUROC), and Cohen’s Kappa, providing a comprehensive assessment of classification quality and scenario-specific improvement.</p><p>Finally, model selection in this study was guided by the need for architectural transparency, reproducibility, and feasibility under limited computational resources. Accordingly, established convolutional and multimodal fusion approaches were prioritized to enable controlled evaluation of fusion and oversampling effects.</p><p>The present analysis intentionally isolates embedding-level resampling after multimodal fusion to examine directional performance changes under a fixed architectural and training configuration. Alternative imbalance mitigation techniques, including weighted loss functions, focal loss, and image-level augmentation, were not included in order to preserve experimental control over the latent-space manipulation. This design does not imply methodological superiority of embedding-level resampling relative to these techniques, but rather isolates its behaviour within the defined protocol.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec5">Dataset description</h3><p>The dataset comprises 3,000 high-resolution images of oral cavities, captured using mobile phone cameras from patients within the Sri Lankan<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 55" title="Piyarathne, N. S. et al. Dataset of annotated oral cavity images for oral cancer detection. Zenodo, https://doi.org/10.5281/ 0664056 (2024). Accessed: 2025-10-29." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR55" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e857">55</a></sup>. It includes documented patient and sample demographics, imaging modality information, and standardized annotation protocols, which are described here to support transparency and reproducibility, while acknowledging that the data originate from a single source and do not involve multi-center harmonization. Each image has been reviewed and categorised by domain experts into one of four clinical classes: Healthy, Benign lesions, Oral Potentially Malignant Disorders (OPMD), and OCA. Figure <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Fig2">2</a> illustrates the distribution of samples across the four diagnostic categories in the oral cancer dataset, with OPMD as the majority class and OCA cases represent only 4.3% of the dataset, underscoring the extreme imbalance.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-2" data-title="Fig. 2"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig2" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 2</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41598-026-52410-0/MediaObjects/41598_2026_52410_Fig2_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-2-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41598-026-52410-0/MediaObjects/41598_2026_52410_Fig2_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 2" loading="lazy" width="685" height="261"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/figures/2" data-track-dest="link:Figure2 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 2" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-2-desc"><p>Histogram showing class distribution in the oral cancer dataset. OPMD is the majority class, while OCA is the most underrepresented.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>In addition to the image data, each sample includes a structured metadata record that captures clinically relevant char- acteristics of the patient. These fields describe the <i>patient’s age</i> at the time of diagnosis and <i>biological sex</i>, together with widely recognised behavioural risk factors linked to malignant transformation in oral tissues. Specifically, the metadata indicate whether the individual currently smokes or has a history of <i>smoking</i>, their <i>alcohol</i> consumption habits, and whether they <i>chew</i></p><p><i>betel-quid</i>, an established contributor to oral cancer in South Asian populations. The metadata also contain the expert-provided diagnostic label associated with the corresponding image. Together, these variables provide essential epidemiological context that complements the visual appearance of lesions and supports more accurate and clinically grounded classification.</p><p>Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab1">1</a> summarises the demographic characteristics and prevalence of key risk factors across the four diagnostic categories in the dataset. The statistics include sample counts, mean age, and proportions of male patients, smokers, betel-quid chewers, and alcohol consumers, providing an overview of population distribution and risk exposure patterns relevant to oral cancer research.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-1"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab1" data-test="table-caption">Table 1 Descriptive statistics by diagnostic category, including demographic and risk factor distributions.</b></figcaption><div class="u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/tables/1" aria-label="Full size table 1"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><p>We confirmed that the class counts reported in Figure <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Fig2">2</a> and Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab1">1</a> are internally consistent, with a total of 3,000 samples. We observed that the OCA group presented a higher mean age compared with other diagnostic categories; however, this observation is reported descriptively and not interpreted within a formal inferential framework.</p><p>In summary, the multimodal structure supports the integration of visual features with clinical metadata for early oral cancer detection, using a consistently annotated dataset drawn from a single institutional source<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Piyarathne, N. et al. A comprehensive dataset of annotated oral cavity images for diagnosis of oral cancer and oral potentially malignant disorders. Oral Oncol. 156, 106946 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1153">24</a></sup>. The availability of aggregated patient metadata, multiple diagnostic classes, and transparent reporting of class distributions enables controlled evaluation of multimodal fusion and imbalance-aware learning strategies. At the same time, the single-source nature of the data is acknowledged as a limitation, providing a clear context for interpretation and motivating future validation on more diverse, multi-institutional datasets.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec6">Feature extraction</h3><p>We extracted high-level visual features from oral cavity images using a CNN based DenseNet121 architecture pretrained on ImageNet. Recent studies have demonstrated the strong performance of DenseNet121 in medical image classification tasks, particularly for oral and dermal lesion analysis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 56" title="Ormeño-Arriagada, P., Navarro, E., Taramasco, C., Gatica, G. &amp; Vásconez, J. P. Deep learning techniques for oral cancer detection: Enhancing clinical diagnosis by resnet and densenet performance. In International Conference on Applied Informatics, 59–72 (Springer, 2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR56" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1165">56</a></sup>. All images were resized to 224 × 224 pixels and normalised to ensure consistent input distribution. The model was truncated before its classification head to preserve the deep convolutional representations learned during pretraining. DenseNet121 was chosen for its efficient feature propagation and strong ability to capture subtle patterns. Feature-level fusion with patient metadata incorporated relevant clinical covariates that may influence lesion characteristics.</p><p>The output of the final convolutional block was passed through a global average pooling layer, which reduced the spatial dimensions and produced a fixed-length image embedding vector of 512 dimensions for each image. These embeddings encode salient visual characteristics such as lesion shape, color intensity, texture, and anatomical context, making them well-suited for downstream classification tasks. The 512-dimensional projection was selected to balance representational richness and model generalisation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 57" title="Lin, Z., Bas, E., Singh, K. Y., Swaminathan, G. &amp; Bhotika, R. Relaxing contrastiveness in multimodal representation learning. In Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF winter conference on applications of computer vision, 2227–2236 (2023)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR57" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1172">57</a></sup>.</p><p>The extracted image embeddings were subsequently concatenated with the corresponding tabular metadata vectors, comprising patient age, sex, and risk factor indicators, to form a joint multimodal feature vector. This fused representation, integrating both visual and clinical information, was then used as input to a fully connected classification head tasked with distinguishing between healthy tissue, benign conditions, OPMD, and confirmed OCA.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec7">Vector fusion</h3><p>Following feature extraction, we performed a late fusion strategy by concatenating the image embeddings with the corresponding patient metadata vectors. The image embedding vectors, obtained from the DenseNet121 architecture, had a dimensionality of 512 and captured rich spatial and semantic information from the oral cavity images. On the other hand, the metadata vectors were derived from structured clinical data, including age, sex, and lifestyle risk factors such as smoking, alcohol use, and betel-quid chewing. Metadata variables were incorporated as additional structured inputs within the fused embedding space. No formal attribution or feature-importance analysis was performed.</p><p>Prior to fusion, categorical metadata features were one-hot encoded, and continuous features were standardized using z-score normalization to ensure numerical compatibility across modalities. The resulting metadata vectors had a dimensionality of <i>d</i><sub><i>m</i></sub>, which was empirically determined based on the number of encoded features.</p><p>The two modalities were then integrated by performing a vector concatenation along the feature axis, yielding a unified multimodal vector of size 512 + <i>d</i><sub><i>m</i></sub>. This fused representation was designed to capture both the morphological characteristics of the lesion and the associated patient-specific risk profile, enabling the model to make more informed predictions. The multimodal vectors were subsequently fed into deep learning classifier for final label prediction.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec8">Data imbalance techniques</h3><p>The dataset exhibited substantial class imbalance, with OCA cases markedly underrepresented compared with OPMD, benign lesions, and healthy controls. To enhance model sensitivity to minority cases, we applied oversampling and cost-sensitive learning within the multimodal feature space, which combined deep CNN embeddings with structured clinical metadata<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Salmi, M., Atif, D., Oliva, D., Abraham, A. &amp; Ventura, S. Handling imbalanced medical datasets: Review of a decade of research. Artif. Intell. Rev. 57, 273 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1214">21</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 58" title="Araf, I., Idri, A. &amp; Chairi, I. Cost-sensitive learning for imbalanced medical data: A review. Artif. Intell. Rev. 57, 80 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR58" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1217">58</a></sup>, improving gains in medical data models<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 59" title="Huang, Y. et al. Privacy-preserving predictive modeling: Harmonization of contextual embeddings from different sources. JMIR Med. Inform. 6, e9455 (2018)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR59" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e1221">59</a></sup>. To correct this imbalance within the fused multimodal representation, we applied three complementary strategies. First, SMOTE generated additional oral cancer examples by interpolating between minority instances and their nearest neighbours in the embedding space, helping expand the decision region for malignant cases. Second, ADASYN further concentrated new samples around difficult boundary areas, strengthening the model’s ability to recognise subtle malignant patterns. Finally, a CW loss function increased the penalty for incorrectly predicting rare cases, ensuring they were prioritised during learning. Applying these methods directly to the fused feature vectors preserves the semantic structure of both visual and clinical information while improving minority-class sensitivity with minimal noise. Together, these imbalance-aware strategies promote more reliable discrimination of malignant lesions in a dataset dominated by benign and premalignant conditions .The strategy used to address class imbalance involved embedding-level oversampling applied after multimodal fusion within the embedding space, increasing minority-class representation without modifying the original images. Evaluation relied on macro-averaged metrics to ensure balanced assessment across classes, while alternative imbalance-mitigation techniques were not explored in order to maintain a controlled analysis.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec9">Classifier models</h3><p>We evaluated four complementary classifiers on fused multimodal feature vectors that combine 512-dimensional DenseNet121 image embeddings with preprocessed patient metadata. Logistic Regression (LR) offered an interpretable linear baseline with regularisation for stability in high-dimensional space. Support Vector Machines (SVM) with a radial basis kernel captured nonlinear boundaries driven by subtle embedding variations. Random Forest (RF) modelled heterogeneous feature interactions through tree ensembles that increase variance-controlled outcome to noise. XGBoost (XGB) further refined decision rules by sequentially boosting trees with explicit regularisation to control overfitting. This methodological diversity enabled a comprehensive assessment of multimodal learning performance and sensitivity to class imbalance in the context of early oral cancer detection. For transparency and reproducibility, full hyperparameter configurations for all classifiers are provided in the Supplementary Materials. Parameters were selected through iterative tuning based on validation performance and established best practices for high-dimensional biomedical data. All models were trained using a consistent optimization and training protocol across experimental configurations. A complete summary of hyperparameter values is provided in Table 5, provided in the supplementary section.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec10">Evaluation</h3><p>We evaluated the models using a stratified 70/30 train–test split repeated across four independent runs, each initialised with a distinct fixed random seed to support reproducibility and controlled estimation of performance variability. In each run, we applied resampling strategies, including class weighting, SMOTE, and ADASYN, exclusively to the training partition in order to prevent information leakage. We retained the same held-out test set across resampling strategies within each run to enable paired comparisons and consistent effect estimation. We report performance as mean ± standard deviation across runs, providing a reliable estimate of generalisation performance under limited and imbalanced data conditions. The evaluation protocol was deliberately designed to examine how model performance behaves under limited data, rather than to draw formal statistical conclusions. We relied on repeated stratified splits to track whether performance patterns remain consistent across runs, offering a practical view of directional stability. That said, this choice carries important limitations. It does not match the robustness of stratified k-fold cross-validation, nor is it intended for hypothesis testing. Instead, the approach should be seen as a pragmatic, exploratory first solution that balances methodological caution with the realities of working in data-constrained settings.</p><p>We assessed model performance using complementary metrics appropriate for imbalanced multi-class classification. Accuracy provided a global measure of correctness, while precision, recall, and macro F1 captured class-balanced behaviour and sensitivity to minority classes. We further quantified discriminative capability using the area under the ROC curve and accounted for chance agreement through Cohen’s Kappa. To examine stable tendency, we analysed descriptive confidence</p><p>intervals across runs, which we interpret as exploratory indicators of performance stability rather than formal statistical significance, given the limited number of repetitions (<i>n</i> = 4). Together, these metrics provide a comprehensive evaluation of both overall performance and the ability to identify clinically relevant malignant cases in the presence of class imbalance.</p><p>In multi-class evaluation, both macro and weighted averaging were applied to summarise performance across classes. Macro averaging treats all classes equally, providing a balanced view of model performance, while weighted averaging accounts for class frequency, offering a more representative estimate under class imbalance. Combining both metrics ensures a comprehensive assessment of model behaviour across prevalent and minority cancer categories. These metrics collectively quantify both overall accuracy and minority-class sensitivity, directly addressing the study’s central hypothesis. All experiments were implemented in Python using standard ML and DL libraries. Model training and evaluation were conducted on a workstation equipped with an AMD Ryzen 7 5800U processor, 16 GB of RAM, and solid-state storage, using integrated graphics rather than a dedicated GPU. The operating system, software framework versions, and hardware specifications are reported to support reproducibility. Random seeds were fixed where applicable to reduce nondeterministic variability across runs.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec11">Reproducibility and resource availability</h3><p>A public repository is provided at <a href="https://github.com/pormen/OCA_Embedding_Fusion">https://github.com/pormen/OCA_Embedding_Fusion</a>, containing all resources required to reproduce the reported experiments, including data preprocessing scripts, multimodal fusion and embedding-level oversampling pipelines, model configuration files with fixed random seeds, and evaluation and metric computation scripts.
