These materials support exact replication of the experimental results and improve methodological transparency.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Results"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec12-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec12">Results</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec12-content"><p>This section presents the experimental results addressing the study’s core hypothesis: whether embedding-level oversampling methods (SMOTE and ADASYN) within the fused image–metadata space improve classification performance—particularly macro recall and macro F1 for OCA, compared with raw fusion, CW training, and unimodal baselines. All models were trained and evaluated under a stratified 70/30 train–test split, repeated across four independent runs with fixed random seeds to ensure reproducibility. Resampling was applied exclusively to the training set in each run to prevent data leakage. The same held-out test set was reused across all imbalance-handling strategies and classifiers, enabling paired within-split comparisons. Performance metrics included accuracy, macro precision, macro recall, macro F1, AUROC, and Cohen’s Kappa, providing a comprehensive evaluation of model repeatable pattern to class imbalance in a multiclass medical imaging context. Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab2">2</a> summarises the mean and standard deviation of test performance across runs for the four classifiers.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-2"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab2" data-test="table-caption">Table 2 Average test performance (mean ± standard deviation across four runs with fixed seeds) under each imbalance-handling strategy.</b></figcaption><div class="u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/tables/2" aria-label="Full size table 2"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><p>Ensemble-based classifiers exhibited performance differences when imbalance-handling strategies were applied. In particular, Random Forest and XGBoost showed increases in macro recall and macro F1 under resampling conditions relative to the raw configuration. Class weighting provided moderate changes, while resampling approaches, including ADASYN, were associated with higher sensitivity to minority classes within the evaluated protocol. These results reflect performance variation under the defined experimental configuration rather than comparative superiority across imbalance-handling strategies.</p><p>Class-wise analysis using XGBoost indicated that embedding-level resampling was associated with increased detection of malignant lesions relative to the raw configuration. Under the imbalanced setting, the model showed limited identification of OCA cases, whereas SMOTE and ADASYN were associated with increases in OCA F1. These changes occurred without substantial degradation in majority-class performance, with benign, healthy, and OPMD categories showing relatively stable macro F1 values across configurations. These observations reflect performance differences within the evaluated dataset rather than evidence of generalisable improvement.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec13">Overall classification performance</h3><p>Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab2">2</a> summarises the average performance of LR, RF, SVM, and XGB under different imbalance-handling strategies. Reported metrics include accuracy, macro-averaged precision, recall, macro F1, AUROC, and Cohen’s Kappa, expressed as mean ± standard deviation across four runs.</p><p>The LR consistently produced the weakest results, indicating that linear models could not capture the complexity of fused image and clinical embeddings. Random Forest improved with resampling methods, particularly ADASYN, which raised its macro F1 compared with the raw condition. SMOTE also enhanced recall and agreement, highlighting the responsiveness of ensemble models to class imbalance. SVM achieved stable accuracy between 0.629 and 0.640, with minimal variation across strategies. Its highest macro F1 occurred under the Raw condition, suggesting limited benefit from resampling. XGB delivered the strongest overall performance. CW yielded the highest accuracy, while ADASYN and SMOTE improved recall and macro F1. XGB also achieved the highest AUROC values and Cohen’s Kappa, confirming its bounded variability across folds under imbalanced conditions.</p><p>Overall, imbalance-handling strategies were associated with performance variation across classifiers, particularly for tree- based models such as Random Forest and XGBoost. Increases in macro recall and macro F1 were observed under resampling conditions relative to raw configurations. These findings should be interpreted as indicative of behaviour under the evaluated experimental protocol, without implying methodological superiority or generalisable advantage.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec14">Improvements in minority class detection</h3><p>Detecting OCA cases is the primary clinical objective of this study. Models trained on raw data showed limited sensitivity (recall) to the minority class. LR and RF frequently misclassified OCA cases, and Support Vector Machine achieved only modest gains when imbalance remained unaddressed.</p><p>Imbalance-handling strategies were associated with increased minority class detection under the evaluated configuration, particularly in tree-based models. With ADASYN, RF recall increased from 0.394 to 0.526, and macro F1 rose from 0.391 to 0.559. XGBoost also benefited, achieving recall of 0.559 with ADASYN compared with 0.488 under raw data. These improvements were further reflected in AUROC and precision–recall curves, where resampling and CW shifted decision boundaries towards better identification of OCA cases while maintaining specificity for non-cancer classes.</p><p>XGB with CW or ADASYN achieved the best balance between sensitivity and overall accuracy. This trade-off is critical in clinical applications, where missing OCA cases poses greater risk than false positives. The results emphasise the importance of imbalance-aware learning for improving diagnostic sensitivity when using fused image and clinical embeddings.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec15">Performance gains from multimodal integration</h3><p>To evaluate the influence of different feature configurations, we trained a SVM classifier using stratified cross-validation across multiple input representations. Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab3">3</a> summarizes the results. The integration of embeddings with metadata achieved the highest accuracy, surpassing all unimodal settings. We evaluated model performance using features extracted through DenseNet121, decreased from 512 to 32, a gradual decline in performance was observed, indicating the importance of preserving richer feature representations. Notably, using metadata alone yielded the lowest accuracy, confirming that demographic and lifestyle attributes provide complementary but insufficient information in isolation. These findings highlight the performance gains achieved through multimodal fusion, where the combination of structured metadata with deep image embeddings enhances controlled variability and improves diagnostic reliability compared to unimodal baselines.The SVM baseline was implemented.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-3"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab3" data-test="table-caption">Table 3 We compared cross-validation accuracy across different feature configurations derived from DenseNet121.</b></figcaption><div class="u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/tables/3" aria-label="Full size table 3"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><p>with an RBF kernel, probability estimates, <i>C</i> = 1<i>.</i>0, <i>γ</i> =  scale, and CW = balances. This model served as the reference to compare the impact of different feature configurations and imbalance handling strategies. All unimodal baselines used identical. Splits, seeds, and hyperparameter search ranges to ensure fair comparison against multimodal fusion.</p><p>The inclusion of both image embeddings and patient metadata was associated with improved model performance, confirming the complementary nature of visual and contextual information in oral cancer classification. The multimodal fusion achieved higher accuracy and greater stability across folds compared with embeddings alone, reflecting improved generalisation and quantified difference. This result supports the hypothesis that integrating heterogeneous data sources provides a more discriminative feature space, enabling more reliable differentiation between lesion types.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec16">Exploratory stability and variance analysis</h3><p>The robustness of the classifiers was examined through descriptive confidence interval analysis across four repeated train/test evaluations. Given the limited number of repeats (<i>n</i> = 4), these analyses are intended as exploratory stability assessments rather than formal statistical significance tests. Narrower intervals indicate greater consistency across runs, while upward shifts reflect improved sensitivity to minority cases. Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Tab4">4</a> reports 95% confidence intervals (CIs) for macro recall and macro F1, which are the most clinically relevant metrics in this context. Wider intervals reflect increased variability in model sensitivity to minority.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-4"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab4" data-test="table-caption">Table 4. 95% confidence intervals for Macro Recall and Macro F1 across classifiers and imbalance-handling strategies. Values represent 95% confidence intervals across four folds.</b></figcaption><div class="u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0/tables/4" aria-label="Full size table 4"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><p>classes. Accordingly, the reported intervals should be interpreted as descriptive indicators of performance stability and effect direction.</p><p>Logistic Regression exhibited wide and overlapping intervals across all strategies, reinforcing its limited ability to capture minority class patterns consistently. Random Forest demonstrated a marked improvement in both recall and macro F1 under SMOTE and ADASYN, with intervals shifting upward relative to the Raw and CW baselines, suggesting that resampling improved reliability for minority class detection. Support Vector Machine maintained relatively stable intervals regardless of strategy, indicating robustness but little gain in minority class sensitivity. XGBoost produced the tightest and highest intervals under imbalance-aware methods, with ADASYN and SMOTE showing recall CIs extending above 0.55 and macro F1 CIs above 0.58, reflecting both improved performance and reduced variance across folds. Together, these findings confirm that ensemble methods, particularly XGBoost, achieve more reliable gains under resampling, whereas linear and margin-based models remain comparatively insensitive to imbalance-handling. The consistency of CI patterns supports the gains of the observed improvements and underscores the importance of combining ensemble learning with targeted imbalance mitigation.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec17">Visualizations of key metrics</h3><p>To further illustrate the impact of data balancing on classification outcomes, Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#Fig3">3</a> presents the row-normalised confusion matrices obtained with the XGBoost models.</p><div Error bars denote standard deviations across four runs.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>Mutual information analysis confirmed that clinical metadata contributes independent discriminative signal, with betel-quid chewing and age showing the strongest associations with diagnostic category. This analysis quantifies statistical association between individual clinical variables and class labels but does not imply causal influence on classifier decisions within the fused latent space. This complementary information supports the performance gains observed in multimodal fusion by providing contextual cues that are not apparent from visual features alone, particularly for high-risk cases where early morphological changes may be subtle.</p><p>These results support the rationale for multimodal fusion. The top clinical predictors provide complementary information to the visual embeddings, which predominantly encode morphological patterns. In practice, integrating these metadata with image-derived features enables the model to condition visual cues on patient context (for example, risk behaviours or age strata), which is consistent with the observed gains in minority-class recall when using imbalance-aware strategies. The relatively modest MI for alcohol and smoking compared with chewing betel-quid suggests heterogeneity in how different behaviours manifest in the data, reinforcing the value of combining multiple clinical covariates rather than relying on any single factor.</p><p>Overall, this evaluation strategy provides a structured descriptive assessment of model behaviour under class-imbalance conditions within the defined experimental setup. Finally, granular error characterization, including confusion matrices, case-level qualitative inspection, and systematic false-negative analysis, was not performed in the present study. While such analyses are valuable in high-stakes diagnostic contexts, the current work prioritizes aggregate class-balanced performance metrics to evaluate imbalance mitigation under controlled conditions.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Discussion"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec18-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec18">Discussion</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec18-content"><p>This study takes a deliberately structured approach to examining how embedding-level imbalance mitigation behaves within a multimodal classification framework, particularly under the constraints of limited data. All findings are interpreted strictly within the boundaries of the experimental protocol. The focus remains firmly on methodological evaluation; it does not extend to formal explainability analyses, case-level diagnostic assessments, or clinically validated interpretations. Any reference to contextual metadata should therefore be understood as structural support within the model, not as evidence of causal influence or feature attribution. In that sense, the study is not intended to claim clinical readiness, inferential superiority, or broad generalisability. Rather, it offers a carefully controlled analysis based on a single-institution multimodal dataset, with conclusions that are explicitly limited to this specific experimental context.</p><p>The present study demonstrates that integrating multimodal learning with imbalance-aware strategies indicates performance differences in the detection of oral cancer from limited and heterogeneous datasets. By fusing image embeddings with structured clinical metadata and applying embedding-level oversampling, the proposed framework achieves consistent increases in recall and macro F1, particularly for minority cancer cases. The inclusion of metadata likely captures risk-factor correlations that complement morphological cues from images, aligning with known clinical patterns of oral carcinogenesis. To enhance clinical interpretability, future work will quantify variable-level contributions and report case-level explanations for positive OCA predictions.</p><p>In oral oncology, lesion morphology alone rarely provides a complete picture of disease risk or progression, as patient- specific factors such as age, sex, tobacco and alcohol use<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 60" title="Eloranta, R. et al. Oral squamous cell carcinoma: effect of tobacco and alcohol on cancer location. Tob. Induc. Dis. 22, 10–18332 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR60" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2727">60</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 61" title="Zygogianni, A. G. et al. Oral squamous cell cancer: Early detection and the role of alcohol and smoking. Head Neck Oncol. 3, 2 (2011)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR61" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2730">61</a></sup>, and anatomical site significantly influence both the onset and biological behaviour of oral lesions. Integrating patient metadata allows the model to capture the interaction between tissue-level changes and external risk factors driving oral carcinogenesis. Tobacco and alcohol use, together with lesion location, strongly influence malignant transformation risk and histopathological severity. Embedding these clinical covariates alongside image features enables multimodal frameworks to better distinguish benign, premalignant, and malignant lesions while provides additional clinically relevant contextual information aligned with known risk factors. This approach provides a controlled methodological analysis by demonstrating that diagnostic accuracy depends not only on model depth but also on effective data representation and integration, ultimately supporting analysis under the evaluated experimental conditions.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec19">Conceptual interpretation</h3><p>The results show that combining patient metadata with image embeddings and applying imbalance-aware oversampling was associated with increased model sensitivity in oral cancer detection. Metadata integration enables the model to encode latent risk factors such as age, sex, tobacco use, and lesion site, complementing the morphological patterns captured by CNN-derived features. This enriched representation supports more discriminative decision boundaries when oversampling is applied. ADASYN improved macro recall by 0.13 and macro F1 by 0.17 in the Random Forest model, while XGBoost achieved consistent gains of about 0.07 across both metrics. These improvements enhance the model’s capacity to detect minority oral cancer cases and reduce false negatives, reinforcing the potential of multimodal, imbalance-aware learning to support earlier and more accurate clinical diagnosis.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec20">Comparison with prior work</h3><p>Compared with previous image-based approaches, the proposed multimodal framework achieves a marked improvement in diagnostic performance. Earlier studies focused on image augmentation or unimodal pipelines<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 62" title="Alosaimi, W. &amp; Uddin, M. I. Efficient data augmentation techniques for improved classification in limited data set of oral squamous cell carcinoma. CMES-Computer Model. Eng. &amp; Sci. 131 (2022)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR62" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2750">62</a></sup>, whereas the present embedding-level fusion integrates clinical metadata such as age, sex, and behavioural risk factors, including betel-quid chewing, smoking, and alcohol consumption—well-established contributors to oral carcinogenesis. Incorporating this contextual information enhances lesion discrimination and provides additional clinically relevant contextual information by aligning model decisions with recognised clinical and epidemiological risk patterns. The embedding-level oversampling strategy further strengthens performance by generating synthetic samples within a semantically consistent representation space, preserving meaningful clinical variability while mitigating class imbalance and redundancy. Ensemble classifiers, particularly XGBoost and Random Forest, achieved higher recall and macro F1, confirming their sensitivity gains over linear models for high-dimensional biomedical data with nonlinear dependencies<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 63" title="Nakach, F.-Z., Idri, A. &amp; Goceri, E. A comprehensive investigation of multimodal deep learning fusion strategies for breast cancer classification. Artif. Intell. Rev. 57, 327 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR63" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2754">63</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 64" title="Toosi, A., Shiri, I., Zaidi, H. &amp; Rahmim, A. Segmentation-free outcome prediction from head and neck cancer pet/ct images: Deep learning-based feature extraction from multi-angle maximum intensity projections (ma-mips). Cancers 16, 2538 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR64" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2757">64</a></sup>. Collectively, these results demonstrate that metadata-driven multimodal learning combined with embedding-level resampling enhances sensitivity and reliability in oral cancer detection. This aligns with recent evidence in head and neck oncology showing that integrating imaging with clinical and molecular data improves diagnostic and prognostic performance<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 65" title="Dörrich, M. et al. A multimodal dataset for precision oncology in head and neck cancer. Nat. Commun. 16, 7163 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR65" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2761">65</a></sup>. Moreover, systematic reviews support that oversampling techniques such as SMOTE improve minority-class sensitivity in medical datasets<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Salmi, M., Atif, D., Oliva, D., Abraham, A. &amp; Ventura, S. Handling imbalanced medical datasets: Review of a decade of research. Artif. Intell. Rev. 57, 273 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2765">21</a></sup>, underscoring the clinical and computational relevance of the proposed framework.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec21">Clinical and computational implications</h3><p>From a computational standpoint, the proposed embedding-level oversampling framework provides a controlled methodolog- ical examination of embedding-level resampling for balancing multimodal medical datasets. Also integrating multimodal embeddings with imbalance-aware strategies enhanced oral cancer detection, particularly for minority classes. Boosting-based models, especially XGBoost, achieved higher recall and macro F1 with SMOTE or ADASYN, confirming the advantage of ensemble learning for high-dimensional biomedical data. Unlike conventional image-space augmentation, which requires costly GPU operations and risks introducing unrealistic artefacts—synthetic resampling in the latent embedding space operates on compact vector representations, reducing training time and memory usage. This approach maintains semantic consistency while enhancing minority-class representation, enabling ensemble models such as XGBoost and Random Forest to learn more robust boundaries in imbalanced conditions. The resulting pipeline is computationally feasible within the experimental environment on standard hardware, particularly in resource-limited settings.</p><p>Recent work has demonstrated the importance of explainability in oral cancer screening systems, including approaches based on informed deep learning and case-based reasoning<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Cimino, M. G. et al. Explainable screening of oral cancer via deep learning and case-based reasoning. Smart Health 35, 100538 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2780">12</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 66" title="Parola, M. et al. Towards explainable oral cancer recognition: Screening on imperfect images via informed deep learning and case-based reasoning. Comput. Med. Imaging Graph. 117, 102433 (2024)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR66" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2783">66</a></sup>. While such methods explicitly address interpretability and clinical transparency, the present study does not incorporate formal explainability mechanisms. Instead, it focuses on performance stability and imbalance mitigation, with explainability-oriented extensions positioned as an essential direction for future investigation.</p><p>Clinically, the improvement in minority-class sensitivity was associated with improved detection under the evaluated experimental conditions by reducing false negatives that often delay treatment and worsen outcomes. These gains are particularly relevant in low- and middle-income settings, where limited access to specialists and imaging resources constrains screening capacity. By combining photographic data with basic patient metadata, the proposed model supports potential AI-assisted triage applications, discussed as exploratory directions rather than validated deployment outcomes, aligning computational innovation with equitable healthcare delivery. Embedding-level oversampling offers a scalable solution for dataset rebalancing without the computational burden of image augmentation, facilitating potential applicability in resource-constrained settings, considered as a prospective direction rather than a validated deployment scenario. As a decision-support tool, the model supports analysis under the evaluated experimental conditions, prioritises high-risk lesions, and strengthens community screening. For researchers, the multimodal framework provides a reproducible foundation for explainable and generalisable AI in oral oncology, advancing interpretability, domain adaptation, and longitudinal analysis of disease progression.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec22">Clinical translation and regulatory considerations</h3><p>Although the present study does not claim readiness for clinical deployment, several considerations would be required prior to translational application. External validation across independent institutions and imaging devices would be necessary to assess robustness under domain shift and demographic variability. Prospective evaluation within real-world clinical workflows would further be required to examine operational stability and diagnostic impact. From a regulatory perspective, AI-based diagnostic support systems must comply with evolving medical device frameworks, including performance monitoring, documentation of model updates, and post-market surveillance. Data governance, bias mitigation, and transparent reporting standards are essential to ensure ethical deployment. These requirements underscore that the current framework represents a methodological investigation under constrained experimental conditions rather than a clinically validated decision-support system.</p><p>Recent literature highlights the importance of federated validation, domain-shift robustness, and ethical auditing in biomedical AI deployment<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 49" title="Sun, Z. et al. K-means-smote-ala-xgboost: Diagnosis of faults in hot-rolled strip steel plates. Alex. Eng. J. 142, 165–184 (2026)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR49" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2801">49</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 50" title="Ahmed, A., Zeng, X., Xi, R., Hou, M. &amp; Shah, S. A. Enhancing multimodal medical image analysis with slice-fusion: A novel fusion approach to address modality imbalance. Comput. Methods Programs Biomed. 261, 108615 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR50" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2804">50</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 54" title="Singh, V. K., Agarwal, D., Gediya, V. K., Rathore, R. S. &amp; Jiang, W. Mitigating class imbalance in forest fire prediction with gan-augmented data fusion. Inf. Fusion 104005 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR54" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2807">54</a></sup>.</p><p>Emerging biomedical AI research increasingly incorporates explainability frameworks, federated validation strategies, and regulatory-aware evaluation pipelines. While such developments are critical for translational deployment, the present work isolates imbalance mitigation behaviour within a constrained multimodal configuration.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec23">Limitations and future directions</h3><p>The present study was conducted under intentionally constrained experimental conditions. These design choices define the scope of inference rather than reflecting methodological deficiency. All conclusions are bounded to the evaluated single-institution dataset and fixed experimental configuration.</p><p>Despite its promising results, this study presents several limitations that guide future research directions. First, the dataset size and single-center origin may constrain generalisability, and although repeated evaluation suggested consistent performance patterns, larger and more diverse cohorts are required to validate generalisability. Inter-observer variability in lesion annotation could also introduce bias in ground truth labels. To address this, future work will conduct multicentric validation involving patients across diverse geographic and clinical contexts, ensuring population and imaging heterogeneity representative of real-world screening conditions. We will also evaluate cross-site generalisation via external test sets and report domain-shift sensitivity (e.g., device type, lighting) using stratified analyses.</p><p>While metadata inclusion provided additional contextual information at the feature level, the model’s internal decision logic remains opaque. We plan to integrate explainable AI tools such as SHapley Additive exPlanation (SHAP) values or integrated gradients to quantify the contribution of each variable, both visual and clinical, to model predictions, thus enhancing clinical trust and transparency.</p><p>Our architecture primarily relies on CNN-derived embeddings. Emerging transformer-based or attention-driven models could better capture contextual dependencies between lesion morphology and patient-level features. Comparative evaluation against multimodal transformers will help determine whether attention mechanisms further improve sensitivity and calibration. External validation and real-world lightweight clinical integration studies will assess the model’s performance under clinical constraints such as variable lighting, device quality, and image noise. The ultimate objective is to evolve this framework into an explainable, low-cost, multimodal diagnostic assistant capable of reducing false negatives and accelerating early oral cancer. detection in community healthcare settings.</p><p>Although multimodal learning improved classification performance, several limitations remain. The dataset represents a narrow demographic and clinical scope, and annotation subjectivity or residual imbalance may affect generalisability. Broader multicentric validation is therefore required to confirm sensitivity gains. Even so, embedding-level multimodal integration was associated with improved detection of rare oral cancer cases within the evaluated dataset by combining morphological and contextual risk factors, a result of clear oncological relevance. The proposed framework provides structured analysis under constrained experimental condition by uniting computational accuracy with clinically meaningful interpretation, reinforcing its methodological relevance within constrained experimental settings in oral cancer detection.</p><p>The absence of detailed visual diagnostics such as confusion matrices or case-level error analyses represents a limitation of the current study. In particular, challenging or borderline cases may exhibit distinct error patterns that are not fully captured by aggregate metrics alone. Nonetheless, the use of macro-averaged measures and stability analysis partially mitigates this limitation by highlighting sensitivity to minority classes. A more granular exploration of error modes will be necessary to support clinical interpretability and trust.</p><p>Translating AI-based diagnostic systems into clinical practice requires careful consideration beyond predictive perfor- mance. Factors such as device heterogeneity, variability in image acquisition protocols, and population differences can substantially affect model generalization. Moreover, regulatory approval pathways, including compliance with medical device regulations and data governance standards, impose additional constraints on model design, validation, and reporting. Ethical considerations—such as transparency, bias mitigation, and the role of human oversight—are particularly critical in clinical decision-support contexts. The present study does not address these challenges directly, but instead provides a methodological foundation upon which such translational evaluations can be systematically built.</p><p>The use of macro-averaged metrics provides a more informative assessment of model behavior under class imbalance, particularly for rare or minority classes that are clinically critical. Performance gains observed under embedding-level oversampling suggest improved sensitivity (recall) to underrepresented cases, although some variability remains due to limited sample availability. While weighted loss functions and data augmentation have shown promise in related studies, their exclusion in the present work allows clearer attribution of observed effects to the fusion and oversampling strategy. Future work will systematically compare these alternative imbalance-handling approaches under larger and more diverse datasets.</p><p>While recent transformer-based and hybrid architectures have demonstrated strong performance in medical imaging tasks, their evaluation often entails increased computational cost and reduced interpretability<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 67" title="Shamshad, F. et al. Transformers in medical imaging: A survey. Med. Image Anal. 88, 102802 (2023)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR67" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2847">67</a></sup>. The present study instead emphasizes methodological clarity and controlled component analysis. Future benchmarking against such architectures will be essential to assess trade-offs between performance, efficiency, and clinical deployability.</p><p>Future work will extend the methodological analysis of the proposed framework through deeper interpretability, evaluation, and component-level assessment<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 68" title="Ahmed, F. et al. Explainable artificial intelligence (xai) in medical imaging: a systematic review of techniques, applications, and challenges. BMC Med. Imaging (2026)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR68" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2854">68</a></sup>. Dedicated explainability techniques, including visual attribution and feature-importance methods, will be integrated to better characterise the influence of image-derived embeddings and clinical metadata on model predictions, complemented by case-based analyses of correct and erroneous classifications. In parallel, richer evaluation protocols will be adopted, incorporating confusion matrices, threshold-based performance curves, and systematic error analysis to enable finer-grained inspection of minority-class behaviour. More comprehensive ablation studies will also be conducted to isolate the contribution of architectural components, preprocessing choices, and imbalance-mitigation strategies, supporting more robust attribution of observed performance gains.</p><p>In addition, future research will prioritise external validation across independent cohorts, imaging devices, and clinical settings to assess performance tendency and generalisability under real-world variability<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 69" title="Suleman, M. U. et al. Assessing the generalizability of artificial intelligence in radiology: A systematic review of performance across different clinical settings. Ann. Med. Surg. 87, 8803–8811 (2025)." href="/articles/s41598-026-52410-0#ref-CR69" id="ref-link-section-d227170504e2862">69</a></sup>. Multicentre and prospective studies will be required to evaluate clinical utility, workflow integration, and longitudinal performance in routine practice. These efforts will be complemented by investigations into alternative imbalance-handling approaches, computational efficiency, and resource-aware lightweight clinical integration, alongside consideration of data governance, ethical safeguards, and regulatory requirements. Together, these steps are essential for advancing the proposed approach toward clinically deployable, trustworthy, and responsible AI systems for oral cancer detection.</p><p>The absence of external validation, multicenter cohorts, and domain-shift testing precludes any claims of generalisability beyond the present dataset. Such evaluations represent necessary future research before translational consideration. . Similarly, the present findings should not be interpreted as evidence that latent-space synthesis is inherently superior to alternative imbalance mitigation strategies, but rather as an empirical observation within a fixed multimodal configuration. In addition, no formal latency benchmarking, memory profiling, or hardware comparison experiments were conducted. Therefore, no claims regarding suitability for mobile or resource-limited deployment can be made based on the present study. Computational observations are limited to feasibility within the experimental environment. Consequently, future investigations will incorporate feature-attribution methods such as SHAP and Grad-CAM, along with confusion matrices and structured false-negative analysis, to examine decision pathways and clinically relevant error patterns. Taken together, these limitations delineate clear directions for future investigation, including external validation while future research will prioritise external multicenter validation across heterogeneous imaging devices and demographic cohorts. Prospective studies designed to evaluate workflow integration, performance drift, and decision-support interaction will be required before translational consideration.</p><p>The novelty of this study does not lie in introducing a new imbalance mitigation algorithm. Instead, it lies in isolating and empirically examining the interaction between embedding-level resampling and multimodal fusion within a clinical image–metadata representation. While oversampling techniques such as SMOTE and ADASYN are well established in tabular domains, their behaviour after multimodal latent-space fusion has not been systematically characterised under constrained medical imaging conditions. The present work therefore contributes a structured empirical analysis rather than an algorithmic innovation. Accordingly, the findings should be interpreted as exploratory methodological observations within a single-institution experimental framework.</p></div></div></section>
                            
                        </div>

id="contributions">Contributions</h3><p>P.O.-A. led the study, designed the methodology, performed the experiments, analysed the data, and wrote the manuscript. C.T., G.M., D.A., V.Z., D.M., D.A and D.R contributed to study design, clinical interpretation, result validation, and manuscript review. All authors approved the final manuscript.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="corresponding-author">Corresponding author</h3><p id="corresponding-author-list">Correspondence to
                <a id="corresp-c1" aria-label="email Pablo Ormeñ o-Arriagada" href="mailto:pablo.ormeno@uvm.cl">Pablo Ormeñ o-Arriagada</a>.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Ethics declarations"><div class="c-article-section" id="ethics-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="ethics">Ethics declarations</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="ethics-content">
              
              
                <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar1">Competing Interests</h3>
                <p>The authors declare no competing interests.</p>
              
              
                <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar2">Code and Reproducibility</h3>
                <p>Source code, configuration files, and scripts to reproduce splits, resampling, and analyses will be available in an open repository upon publication (link to be provided), enabling full computational reproducibility.</p>
              
              
                <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar3">Use of Artificial Intelligence</h3>
                <p>Large language models were used to assist in language polishing and formatting. The authors are fully responsible for the content and interpretation of all results.</p>
              
              
                <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar4">Ethics and Data Governance</h3>
                <p>This study used de-identified images and metadata from a public repository with appropriate usage permissions (see<sup>55</sup>). No protected health information was processed. We complied with dataset licence terms and institutional guidelines for secondary data analysis.</p>
